# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is the longest sūrah of the Qur'an and the first of the Madinan sūrahs. Al-Baghawī opens his commentary on it with the disagreements about its length and its place: it was revealed after the migration, verse by verse over years rather than in one delivery. The majority of the early scholars held the whole of it to be Madinan. Al-Qurṭubī notes that it was revealed in scattered stretches and that some of its verses came late in the mission, including the verse about the day of return, which he says was the last verse sent down from the sky, revealed at Minā during the farewell pilgrimage. Ibn Kathīr and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān both treat that spread as important, because a community being built in a new city needed its laws delivered as the situations arose.

The sūrah has several names in the books. Al-Baghawī and al-Qurṭubī record that Khālid b. Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qur'an, and al-Alūsī repeats the name and gives a reason: the sūrah gathers rulings that appear nowhere else, and a student can camp in it for a long time. Al-Qurṭubī adds that ʿUmar learned this sūrah with the understanding it contains over twelve years, which tells a reader what kind of book he has opened. Ibn Kathīr records the report of Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet ﷺ said that homes should not be turned into graves, and that Shayṭān does not enter a house in which this sūrah is recited, a report that Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim carries among others. The believers were called out by its name on the day the community faced its enemy, as Ibn Kathīr notes when he lists what the sūrah did in its own time.

The shape of the sūrah is described by al-Saʿdī and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān in the same way. It opens with four verses that describe the believers, two verses that describe those who openly refuse, and then a long passage on the people who say one thing and mean another. After that the sūrah turns to the story of the Children of Israel, because the new community in Madīnah was living beside the heirs of an earlier scripture. It draws the lessons of that history for readers who were about to repeat it. The rest of the sūrah is law: prayer, fasting, pilgrimage, marriage, debts, the prohibition of interest, and the ethics of a community that has to hold together.

Al-Qurṭubī describes the sūrah as a store of rulings and warnings, and al-Alūsī reports the saying that a thousand commands are gathered inside it. Ibn ʿUthaymīn taught students that the whole religion sits in its opening verses: belief in what cannot be seen, established prayer, and spending out of what God has provided. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the sūrah teaches a reader to expect the practical: a book that begins with belief ends with a prayer for forgiveness and a supplication, and between those two points it tells a community how to live in a city, deal with its neighbours, lend its money, raise its children, and settle its disputes.

The sūrah also carries its own warning. Ibn Kathīr notes that the patterns it describes are repeated in every community, so that a Muslim who reads about the people of the earlier books is reading about a road he can still walk down. Al-Ṭabarī, in the same spirit, keeps the sūrah's stories tied to the plain sense of the words rather than to elaborate tales, and al-Baghawī records the early scholars saying that the sūrah's stories are lessons before they are history. A reader who arrives at Al-Baqarah receives a book of law, a book of history and a book of warning, and the three are joined in the same thread.
---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**A SURAH THAT OPENS WITH LETTERS**

Al-Baqarah begins with three letters that are pronounced one by one, with no vowel joining them: ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”***. They are read as letters rather than as a word, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gives them the name by which students of the Qur'an know them, the letters cut off from the rest of the word, because each one stands alone when it is recited. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report that carries his name, understands them line by line: the first letter stands for the name Allāh, the second for Jibrīl, the third for Muhammad, so that the opening spells the three parties in the delivery of the revelation.

The same report gives two other readings of the three letters. One takes them as the beginnings of three of God's names, so that the opening of the sūrah is a reminder of the One who is worshipped, the One who is subtle in His kindness, and the One who is glorious in His majesty. The other takes them as the first letters of His blessings, His kindness and His dominion. The report ends with a fourth view held by many of the earliest believers: the meaning rests with God alone. Al-Jalālayn keeps to that line in his one sentence on the verse, that God knows best what He means by these letters.

Al-Saʿdī takes the same road and explains why it is the safe one. He writes that a believer should say nothing about the letters without evidence from revelation, because guessing about God's speech is a serious matter. That silence is not the silence of a person who thinks the letters mean nothing. Al-Saʿdī insists that God did not send them as play; they were sent for a wisdom, and the fact that a reader cannot see the wisdom does not empty the words of purpose.

Ibn Kathīr collects the same caution from the earliest generation. He reports that Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd all held that this knowledge is kept with God, and he adds that God did not reveal anything in His Book for amusement. The Book itself divides its verses into clear ones and elusive ones: (3:7 — **“some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive”**). Ibn Kathīr and al-Alūsī both note that the openings of sūrahs belong to the second group, and both warn that a reader should not use them as a playground.

**WHAT THE TEN BOOKS DO WITH THREE LETTERS**

Al-Ṭabarī begins with the reports rather than with his own view. He lists those who said that these letters are a name among the names of the Qur'an, from Qatāda, Mujāhid and Ibn Jurayj. He lists those who said they are openings with which God begins His Book. He lists a group who said they are simply letters of the alphabet, and a fourth group who read them as numbers, since each Arabic letter carries a value in the old reckoning. Then he weighs them, which is his method everywhere: the report first, the judgement after it.

His own reading is that the openings are God praising Himself before the story begins. He notes that the Book opens some sūrahs with praise, some with glorification and some with these letters, and that the purpose is the same in each case, to put the servant in the right posture before he hears a command. A person who opens a letter from a king with the king's name in mind reads the rest differently from a person who opens it thinking about himself.

Al-Baghawī gathers the sayings of the first generation on the same point. Abū Bakr is reported to have said that every book has a secret, and that the secret of the Qur'an lies in the openings of its sūrahs. ʿAlī is reported to have said that every book has a chosen portion, and that the chosen portion of this Book is the letters of its spelling. Al-Baghawī draws the practical conclusion from those sayings: the first duty of a reader facing these letters is to believe them, and the second is to leave the meaning to God.

Al-Alūsī collects the opinions at length and then steps back. He says that what lies behind the letters is most likely a hidden knowledge, a secret that the scholars could not reach, and he quotes al-Shaʿbī saying that this is God's secret and that people should not search for it. He then draws a picture worth holding: obedience to a command whose wisdom a person cannot see is itself a form of submission, the same submission that carries a pilgrim through rituals whose reasons he does not grasp. He quotes a line of poetry in which the speaker says that if God ordered him to stand on burning coals, he would stand without hesitating.

**THE LETTERS, AND THEN THE BOOK**

The letters are followed at once by a sentence about a book, and al-Ṭabarī notes the grammar that ties them together. The opening reads as a single statement: the letters, then the Book that has been sent down. The Book is built out of the same twenty-eight letters that every Arab child learns. The sūrah goes on to invite anyone who doubts it to produce a single chapter like it: (2:23 — **“then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true”**). Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the sequence as a quiet challenge, since the raw material of the Book lies in every reader's mouth while the Book itself has no rival.

Al-Ṭabarī records a scene that shows the letters doing their work. Abū Yāsir b. Akhṭab passed by the Prophet ﷺ while he was reciting the opening of this sūrah, and went straight to his brother, the chief of the Jewish tribe of Madīnah, to tell him what he had heard. The letters had caught the ear of a man who came to argue, and he carried the sound of them away with him. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both tell the story in their reading of this passage.

Al-Baghawī notes that the sūrah was called the pavilion of the Qur'an because of its size and the rulings gathered inside it, and al-Qurṭubī adds the report that ʿUmar spent twelve years learning it with its understanding. Ibn Kathīr records the report from Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet ﷺ told his companions not to turn their homes into graves, because the house in which this sūrah is recited is not entered by Shayṭān, and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim carries that report. Ibn Masʿūd is reported in the same place saying that Shayṭān leaves the house where the sūrah is heard.

Imagine two workshops on the same street, using tools anyone can buy. One turns out furniture that no other workshop can copy, and customers cross the city to reach it. The letters of this sūrah are the tools; the Book is the work. Al-Saʿdī ends his note on the opening with the same thought in plain words: the letters remind a reader that the Qur'an came in his own language. The sūrah that follows shows him that the language is not the source of its power.
---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**THE BOOK THAT NEEDS NO INTRODUCTION**

The letters are followed by a statement about the Book itself: ***“This is the Book!”***. Al-Ṭabarī reports that the great majority of the commentators read the opening word as a way of saying 'this is the Book', and he lists Mujāhid, ʿIkrima, al-Suddī and Ibn ʿAbbās among them. He then answers the grammarian's question, because a reader of Arabic expects the word for 'that' to point at something distant. His answer is that the distant form honours what it names, so the sentence lifts the Book before the reader's eyes instead of dropping it in front of him.

Al-Jalālayn gives the same reason in a few words: the demonstrative is there to magnify. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the reports that bear his name, carries the sense into three readings. The first is that the Book is what Muhammad ﷺ is reciting to his people, a text in their own hearing. The second is that the phrase points to the guarded tablet, the source from which revelation comes. The third is that it refers to the Book God promised to send down on the day of the covenant, so that what the reader holds is the arrival of an old promise rather than a new idea.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn works through the sentence the way he taught grammar to his students. He notes that the words can be read in two ways: either the Book is a description of what is being pointed at, so that the denial of doubt stands as the main statement, or the Book is the main statement and the denial of doubt describes the Book's condition. Whichever way a reader takes it, the conclusion he reaches is the same: there is no doubt that the Book is from God, and no doubt about what the Book contains.

Al-Saʿdī reads the sentence as a claim about rank, since the Book described here is the Book in reality, holding what the books of earlier and later ages do not hold. Imagine a letter arriving from a king whose seal a reader knows, carried by a courier he recognises. He does not begin by asking whether the letter is real; he opens it and reads. Al-Baghawī notes that the sūrah treats its audience as people in just that position, and that the Book comes with its author already known.

**NO DOUBT, WHICH IS THE SAME AS CERTAINTY**

The sentence then says of the Book: ***“There is no doubt about it”***. Al-Saʿdī draws a rule from it that runs through the whole Qur'an. When God denies a fault in something He praises, the denial is not empty; it carries the opposite quality, and the opposite of doubt is certainty. So the Book is not merely free of uncertainty; it is the source of certainty, and it removes the doubt of the person who reads it properly. Al-Qurṭubī makes the same point from the side of the language, noting that the Arabic word carries a suspicion that unsettles the heart, and that the Book leaves no room for that kind of unrest.

Al-Ṭabarī supports the reading from the words themselves. He notes that the Book makes claims about God, about the unseen, and about the future, and that a person who accepts those claims finds no wavering in them. Al-Alūsī records the view of the scholars who read the phrase as a statement about the Book's own clarity. Ibn Kathīr adds the plain sense that the denial of doubt concerns both the Book's source and its contents, since every report in it is true and every command in it is just.

The Book also warns that doubt tends to travel towards the parts of revelation that can be twisted. The verse that divides the clear verses from the elusive ones describes the pattern: (3:7 — **“Those with deviant hearts follow the elusive verses seeking ˹to spread˺ doubt through their ˹false˺ interpretations—but none grasps their ˹full˺ meaning except Allah”**). Ibn Kathīr and al-Alūsī both use that verse when they explain why a reader must approach these sūrah openings with humility rather than with a table of guesses. The doubt that harms a person is often not doubt in the Book; it is doubt he brought with him and went looking for a text to carry it.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the claim of the verse is a test of the reader, since a book that denies doubt in itself invites the question of what the reader will do with his questions. Al-Saʿdī states the practical shape of that test: the same Book that guides one reader leaves another in his own confusion. The difference is not in the Book but in the fear of God that the reader brings to it. That fear is the subject the sūrah names next, and it is the reason the sentence about doubt is followed at once by a sentence about guidance.

**GUIDANCE THAT LANDS ON THE MINDFUL**

The verse ends with ***“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. Al-Jalālayn defines such a person as one who guards himself by doing what God has commanded and leaving what He has forbidden, so that he builds a fence around himself in advance. Al-Ṭabarī reports the early scholars explaining the same word as fear of God's punishment and the avoidance of His anger. A person who has ever driven on a road with a cliff on one side understands the picture in a moment: the careful driver is not a coward, he is a man who knows where the drop is.

Al-Saʿdī explains the wording of the verse with care. He notes that God called the Book guidance for people in one place and guidance for the mindful in another, and that the two statements do not conflict. The Book is guidance for everyone in itself, since it shows the truth and warns against error, and it becomes guidance for a particular reader when he takes it up with the fear of God. The Book supplies what he needs to see; his fear supplies the willingness to act on what he sees.

Ibn Kathīr records a conversation that has been repeated in sermons for fourteen centuries. ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb asked Ubayy b. Kaʿb what fear of God means, and Ubayy asked him whether he had ever walked a path with thorns on it. ʿUmar said yes. Ubayy asked what he had done, and ʿUmar said he had rolled up his sleeves and picked his way carefully. Ubayy said that this was the meaning, and the picture has stayed with readers since, because it turns a large word into a way of walking.

The Book states the blessing of that care in a promise: (8:29 — **“If you are mindful of Allah, He will grant you a standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺”**). Ibn ʿUthaymīn taught that guidance comes in two parts, the showing and the enabling, and that the mindful reader receives both. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that this is why the sūrah describes the believers before it describes anybody else, since a reader needs to see the model before he is shown the warnings. The next verse opens that description, and the three marks it names are the three the sūrah keeps returning to.
---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**BELIEVING IN WHAT NO EYE HAS SEEN**

The first mark of the mindful is the one the verse puts first: ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”***. Al-Ṭabarī collects the early readings of the unseen. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that it means what came from God, that is, everything God sent down. In a second report, from al-Suddī through Abū Mālik and Abū Ṣāliḥ from Ibn ʿAbbās, and through Murrah from Ibn Masʿūd, the unseen is what is hidden from the servants, the affairs of the garden and the fire and what God has mentioned in the Qur'an. Qatāda is reported to have listed the garden, the fire, the raising after death and the day of judgment. Zirr b. Ḥubaysh is reported to have said, in the shortest of the reports, that the unseen is the Qur'an itself.

Al-Qurṭubī lays the opinions side by side and then reports the conclusion of Ibn ʿAṭiyya, that they do not contradict each other, because the word covers all of them. He adds his own note that this is the faith described in the report of Jibrīl's questions, which Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim carries, where the Prophet ﷺ told his questioner that faith is to believe in God, His angels, His books, His messengers and the Last Day. A reader who wants to know what the verse is asking of him has the list in front of him, and it is a long one.

Al-Saʿdī explains why this mark comes before the others. He writes that the real test of a person is not whether he accepts what his eyes has shown him, since nobody argues with the sunrise. The test is whether he accepts what he has never seen on the word of God and His messenger. That acceptance is what separates the believer from the one who denies, because it is trust built on testimony rather than on sight. It is the part of faith that a person can carry out of this life with him. Ibn Kathīr defines faith in the same way, as complete assent that takes the limbs along with it, which is why the verse moves from belief to action in the same breath.

Imagine a worker who takes a job in a city he has never visited because the offer came from someone he trusts. He packs his bag, learns the language, and leaves his family on the strength of a promise. Nobody would call that foolishness if the offer was solid. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that the believer who trusts God's word in the same way is the person the verse is describing. Al-Saʿdī notes that the Qur'an supplies him with the details he cannot see, so that his journey has a destination before he has a map.

**PRAYER THAT IS ESTABLISHED, NOT MERELY PERFORMED**

The same verse names the second mark: prayer that is set up and kept standing. The words are chosen carefully, and Ibn Kathīr collects the explanation of them from the first generation. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said through ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭalḥah that the phrase means performing the prayer with everything that belongs to it, and in another report he says it means completing the bowings, the prostrations, the recitation, the humility and the presence of heart. Qatāda says it means keeping the prayer at its times and with its ablution. Muqātil b. Ḥayyān says it means keeping its time, completing its purity, and finishing its bowings and prostrations.

Al-Ṭabarī reports the same understanding with a small and useful detail: the ones who establish the prayer in this verse pray while the matter of the earlier communities is still fresh in the sūrah's story. Their prayer is not a gesture made for people to see. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse uses the noun of standing rather than the noun of the act, so that the prayer is described as a structure in a life rather than as an event in a day. Al-Baghawī reads the phrase as a description of people whose prayer comes first in their schedule.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn drew attention to the little word 'and' that joins belief to prayer, and used it as a lesson about the order of a religious life. Belief sits in the heart, prayer stands in the body, and spending leaves the hand, and as-Saʿdī observes that the verse walks a person through the three in that order. The Book repeats the pairing of prayer and giving in a way a reader can see for himself: (98:5 — **“establish prayer, and pay alms-tax. That is the upright Way”**), and again in the words of the Prophet's own devotion, (6:162 — **“Surely my prayer, my worship, my life, and my death are all for Allah—Lord of all worlds”**).

Imagine a shop that opens at the same hour every day for twenty years, through heat and through rain, and let the reader compare it with a shop that opens when the owner feels like opening. The first shop becomes part of the street, and neighbours set their watches by it. Al-Saʿdī says that the believer's prayer is meant to work the same way in his own schedule, so that the five appointments hold his day together instead of being squeezed into whatever is left over.

**SPENDING FROM WHAT WAS NEVER REALLY OURS**

The verse closes with ***“and donate from what We have provided for them”***. Al-Ṭabarī reports the strongest reading of the phrase in his own words: the best and most deserving interpretation is that these people were fulfilling every due that their wealth carried, whether it was the alms-tax or the maintenance of the family members whose upkeep was on them. He argues from the fact that God praised them for the act without limiting it to one kind of spending, so the praise covers all the kinds that earn a reward.

Ibn Kathīr records the reports behind that reading. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that the phrase means the alms-tax owed on their wealth, while al-Suddī, reporting from Abū Mālik and Abū Ṣāliḥ from Ibn ʿAbbās and from Murrah from Ibn Masʿūd, says it means a man's spending on his household before the alms-tax was made obligatory. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk says that charitable spending was a free offering at first, according to a person's ability, until the obligations came and set the shares. Ibn Kathīr draws the conclusion that the Book, in this verse and elsewhere, ties prayer and spending together because the first is a right owed to God and the second is a kindness owed to His creatures.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the wealth is described in the verse as something God provided, which changes the way a person holds it. A man who treats his earnings as a sum he created will find spending a loss; a man who treats them as provision handed to him will find spending a return of trust. Ibn ʿUthaymīn taught that this is why the Book calls the act a lending to God in another place, since what a person gives is returned to him by the One who had given it in the first place. The Book keeps the same arrangement in front of a reader elsewhere: (9:103 — **“Take from their wealth ˹O Prophet˺ charity to purify and bless them, and pray for them—surely your prayer is a source of comfort for them”**), and in the warning that says, (63:10 — **“And donate from what We have provided for you before death comes to one of you”**).

Imagine a manager who is handed a company card and told to buy whatever the office needs. He would not think of the balance as his own money, and he would not hesitate to spend it on the people the company serves. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws that picture out of the verse and its ordering: the sūrah begins its description of the believers with a heart that trusts, moves to a body that prays, and finishes with a hand that gives. Al-Baghawī notes that the three together are what the next verse calls guidance, and that a reader who wants to know whether he is among the people this sūrah describes has a short list to check.
---

## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**THE CONVERTS WHO BROUGHT THEIR OLD BOOKS WITH THEM**

The description of the believers continues with a fourth mark: ***“and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺”***. Al-Qurṭubī records the disagreement over who is meant. One group of scholars held that the previous verse describes the believers who came out of the Arabs, and that this verse describes the believers who came out of the People of the Book, naming ʿAbdallāh b. Salām as the man about whom it was revealed. Another group held that both verses describe the whole community of believers. Al-Ṭabarī settles on the reading that ties the two verses together, because the sūrah turns from the believers of one people to the believers of another, and then turns to those who refused.

What makes the verse remarkable is the audience it was spoken to. Al-Qurṭubī preserves the report of how the People of the Book answered the earlier verses: they agreed when they heard about belief in the unseen, they agreed when they heard about prayer and spending, and then the request to believe in what was sent down to Muhammad ﷺ reached their ears. At that point they stopped. The Book describes their answer in their own words elsewhere: (2:91 — **“We only believe in what was sent down to us,” and they deny what came afterwards, though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures”**). The verse was a line in the sand, and it was drawn by the reach of one small phrase.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the two kinds of early believers with a report from Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās. The first were people who had worshipped idols and then accepted Islam, and the earlier verses describe them. The second were people raised in the scriptures of the Jews and the Christians who accepted Islam after the Prophet ﷺ came, and this verse describes that second group. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān adds that these converts were promised a double reward, once for believing in the scripture they were raised on, and once for believing in the final Book when it reached them. Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim both carry that promise from Abū Mūsā.

Imagine a family that has kept an old land deed for three generations, and then receives an official copy of the same deed from the court, corrected and sealed. The family that trusts the court files both. The family that has been treating the old copy as its private treasure refuses the new one at the door. Al-Saʿdī notes that the verse praises the family that files both, and that its praise is aimed at a habit of mind rather than at a bloodline.

**THE EARLIER BOOKS, AND WHAT BELIEVING IN THEM MEANS**

The verse then names the earlier revelation: ***“and what was revealed before you”***. Al-Jalālayn identifies the books as the Torah, the Gospel and the other scriptures sent before the Qur'an. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have described the mark as belief in the scriptures given to all the prophets, which is a wider circle than the two books a reader might think of first. Al-Ṭabarī reports that the early scholars read the phrase as a description of people who accept the messengers as one line, with no picking and choosing among them.

Ibn Kathīr draws out what that belief means for a Muslim who lives after the final revelation. He writes that the Muslim believes the earlier books were sent by God, and that the Qur'an is the book that confirms them and stands over them as the guardian. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the same place, says that these believers did not make distinctions between the messengers and did not reject what the messengers brought from their Lord. Al-Baghawī records the same understanding in a sentence worth remembering: the Muslim's belief in the earlier books is belief that God sent them, not belief that every page now in circulation is exactly as it left the prophet's hand.

Al-Qurṭubī goes further and asks whether the phrase obliges a Muslim to know the earlier books themselves. His answer is that the obligation is belief in their origin and in what they contained of the truth, while the details are governed by the Qur'an. Al-Alūsī records the same position and notes that the Book itself takes this line when it praises those who believe in what was sent down to the Prophet ﷺ and before him. The point is not that all books are equal. It is that revelation is one chain, and a person who believes in it does not cut the chain behind him to hold on to the last link.

Imagine a town that receives a new map of the roads from the same office that drew the old one. The old map was true for its own time, and the new map corrects what has changed. A traveller carrying both is not confused; he simply trusts the latest edition for the road he is on. Al-Saʿdī ends his reading of the phrase with the same thought, that the believer's confidence rests on the sender of the revelation rather than on the age of the paper in his hand.

**CERTAINTY, WHICH IS MORE THAN AGREEMENT**

The mark closes with ***“and have sure faith in the Hereafter”***. Ibn ʿAbbās lists what the certainty covers: the resurrection after death, the standing for account, the garden, the fire, the reckoning and the scale on which deeds are weighed. He adds that the verse especially concerns ʿAbdallāh b. Salām and the believers who came with him from the earlier scriptures, a group whose conviction had already been tested once in their own community. Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading plain: they know that the Hereafter is real.

Al-Saʿdī explains why the Hereafter is named on its own after the general mention of revelation. He writes that belief in the last day is a pillar of faith on its own, and that it is the greatest driver of both desire and fear in a person's life. He also defines the certainty named here: complete knowledge that leaves no space for doubt and that pushes a person to act. This is why the word used is not agreement or opinion. A man may agree that he will die one day; a man who is certain prepares for it.

Al-Baghawī places this verse beside the report of Jibrīl's questions to the Prophet ﷺ, which Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim carries, where faith is described as belief in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, the resurrection after death, the garden, the fire and the decree of good and evil. Ibn Kathīr notes that the same list appears in the Book in different arrangements, and that the sūrah here folds the whole of it into a single description of the people it is praising. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān adds the observation that the believers of the earlier books are mentioned a second time in this list because their path to faith was harder: they had to leave a community behind.

Imagine a student who has an examination date written on his wall, and a second student who admits that the date may be real. Both may say the same sentence about the examination, and only one of them studies. Al-Qurṭubī draws the practical conclusion from the verse: the reward promised to the believer is tied to a certainty that shows itself in his calendar and his purse, not to a word he is willing to repeat. Al-Alūsī reads the certainty as a light that the servant carries into the day, and the next verse says what happens to people who walk by it.
---

## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**A VERDICT ON THE PEOPLE JUST DESCRIBED**

The four marks of the believers end with a sentence of judgement: ***“It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as God settling the matter for the reader, so that the people described in the previous lines are identified as the people of guidance and success, and nobody else can claim the title by resemblance. He notes that the verse comes after the mention of converts from the earlier books on purpose, so that the claim of a bloodline or an inherited book is set aside and the claim of faith is honoured.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the guidance as favour, mercy and clear exposition from the Lord, and the success as escape from God's anger and punishment. Al-Jalālayn says that the people described will enter the garden and be saved from the fire. Ibn Kathīr explains the guidance as light and insight, and adds that the success belongs to them in this world and in the next, because they obtain what they seek and are saved from what they fear. The four books agree on the shape of the sentence: the guidance is present, and the success is stored.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the place of the sentence in the sūrah's design. The opening verses have described one group of people in detail, and this sentence closes the portrait with a stamp before the sūrah turns to the people who refuse. A reader is meant to finish the description of the believers with the taste of success in his mouth, because the two groups that follow are described so that he can check himself against them. Ibn Kathīr records the observation of Mujāhid that four verses of this sūrah describe the believers, two describe those who openly refuse, and thirteen describe the people whose mouths and hearts disagree.

Imagine a teacher who marks two papers at the end of a course. One paper shows that the student has understood the material; the other shows that he has copied the headings without the work. The marks do not create the difference, they reveal it. Al-Saʿdī puts the same thought in his reading of the sentence: the verses describe the marks, and the sentence that closes them tells the reader where those marks lead, so that the description stops being a list of qualities and becomes a promise he can walk towards.

**ON GUIDANCE, NOT MERELY NEAR IT**

The verse then repeats the pronoun before the closing word: ***“and it is they”*** — and finishes the sentence with ***“who will be successful”***. The repetition has been studied closely, and al-Saʿdī gives the reason for it. He notes that the first sentence says the people are on guidance, using a word that suggests standing above something, while the Book describes the people of misguidance as sunk inside their error. He quotes the verse in which the argument between two groups is left open for the reader to judge: (34:24 — **“certainly one of our two groups is ˹rightly˺ guided; the other is clearly astray”**).

The difference in the Arabic is not decoration. A guided person is described as carried by his guidance, raised by it and moving with it, the way a rider sits above the road he travels. A person in error is described as drowned in it, unable to see over the surface. Al-Saʿdī draws the contrast into a picture a reader can use: two men walk into the same market, and one of them has a lamp. The lamp does not change the market. It changes what the man can do inside it.

Ibn Kathīr notes that the noun for guidance is left without a description, and that this absence is itself a way of magnifying it, since the reader is left to imagine a guidance whose size is not measured by anything he has seen. Al-Baghawī and al-Alūsī record the same observation, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn taught his students that a person should read the sentence and then ask which guidance he is walking on, not merely which opinions he holds. The Book ties guidance to a road in hundreds of places, and a road is something a person is on rather than something he owns.

Al-Qurṭubī draws a warning out of the same construction. He notes that the sentence does not say the people have guidance, as though it were a possession they could put in a drawer. It says they are on guidance, which is a position that a person has to keep by moving. A man who stops walking on a road does not stay on it by standing still; he is simply standing on the spot where he stopped. The next verses show two groups of people who did exactly that, and the sūrah spends more time on them than on the believers, because a reader is more likely to need the warning.

**WHAT SUCCESS LOOKS LIKE IN THE QUR'AN'S WORDS**

The closing word of the verse is the one that names the outcome. Al-Saʿdī defines it as obtaining what a person seeks and escaping what he fears, and notes that the Book confines this success to the people described, because there is no other road to it. Ibn Kathīr gives the same definition and spells out the contents: the reward of God, an eternal life in the garden, and safety from the punishment prepared for those who refused Him. Al-Jalālayn reduces it to the two movements of the day of judgement, entering the garden and being saved from the fire.

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have given a third reading that fits an ordinary life: the successful ones are those who find what they were looking for and escape the evil they were fleeing. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the word was used by the Arabs for a farmer who gets his crop and by a man who finds water in a dry country, which is why the Book's use of it is so easily understood by people whose lives depend on land. Al-Qurṭubī records the same origin of the word and adds that the Book borrowed it because every person is managing some kind of land.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān closes the description by looking forward. The sūrah has described the believers, and it now turns to those who persist in refusal before it turns to the people whose hearts and tongues disagree. A reader who has just read a promise wants to know whether he is standing inside it, and the check the sūrah offers is not a feeling. It is the list of five things he has just read: trust in what he cannot see, prayer kept standing, wealth spent, revelation accepted as one chain, and certainty about the account.

Imagine a farmer who reads the weather reports, ploughs at the right time, plants good seed and waters in season. When the harvest comes, the harvest does not make him a farmer; it announces that he was one. The Book uses that announcement here, and Ibn Kathīr notes that the same word is used for the people of the garden in the longest description of the hereafter, so that a reader who plants in this life meets the word again at the gate. The next verse shows what happens to the land that is never ploughed.
---

## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**THE PEOPLE THE WARNING CANNOT REACH**

The sūrah turns from the people it has praised to the people who refuse: ***“As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them”***. Al-Ṭabarī collects the identifications made by the earliest teachers and then chooses one. Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said the verse describes the leaders of the factions, the men who fought and fell at Badr. Others said it describes the hard core of the idolaters of Makkah. Al-Ṭabarī's own preference follows the flow of the sūrah: the previous verses praised the believers among the People of the Book, so the verse that follows describes those of the same people who refused, because the passage is addressed to the children of Israel and to their learned men.

Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report carried under his name, narrows the group to the men who had settled into their refusal: Abū Jahl, Abū Lahab and others like them. He also gives the reports a second reading, that the verse states what God already knew about them, so that no reader should expect the warning to change their position. Ibn Kathīr explains the key word in the verse as the act of covering something up, since the Arabic root describes a person who hides the truth he has recognised. The people in view are not ignorant of the message; they are hiding it.

Al-Saʿdī describes the condition the verse names as a quality that has soaked into them, so that no warning turns them back and no counsel makes an impression. He then draws the consequence for the Prophet ﷺ with unusual tenderness: the verse ends the hope that these particular men will accept, and it does so in order to release the messenger from grief over them. A man who believes that every refusal is a failure of his own will spend his life under a weight God never put on him.

Imagine a man who has been told a bridge is closed, and who keeps driving towards it because the bridge has always been open. The warning he receives is not a failure of the person who gave it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the sūrah describes these people immediately after the description of the believers, and that the contrast is meant to sharpen a reader's sense of what faith costs. Nothing about the two groups is accidental; the sūrah is teaching a person to read himself before he reads anyone else.

**A COMFORT FOR THE ONE WHO CALLS**

The sentence continues with ***“or not they will never believe”***, and the wording is the point of the verse. Al-Ṭabarī notes that God addresses the Prophet ﷺ here with a statement about the limits of warning, and that this does not cancel the duty of delivering the message. The Book repeats the same sentence in another sūrah in the same words, which is why Ibn Kathīr treats it as a settled principle rather than a local remark: (36:10 — **“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”**).

Ibn Kathīr supports the point with a pair of verses that show the principle at work. God says of the people whose sentence has already been passed, (10:96 — **“those against whom Allah’s decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe”**). God says of the People of the Book that even a full display of proofs would not move them: (2:145 — **“Even if you were to bring every proof to the People of the Book, they would not accept your direction ˹of prayer˺”**). Ibn Kathīr draws the conclusion that a teacher should not measure his message by the number of people who accept it, and that the duty of the messenger ends with the delivery.

Al-Saʿdī reads the same sentence as a comfort with two edges. The first edge removes the burden of responsibility for another person's choice. The second edge removes any excuse for not speaking, since the instruction to warn remains in the text beside the news that the warning will not land on certain ears. Ibn ʿUthaymīn made the same point in his lessons: the message is delivered to everyone, and the outcome is not handed to the deliverer as a score.

Imagine a lifeguard on a beach who is required to keep calling out even after one swimmer has swum past every rope and refused the whistle. The calls are not for the man who has decided to drown; they are for everyone still within the sound of the voice, and they are also the duty of the guard. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse's shape returns throughout the sūrah, where a hard statement about one group is followed by an invitation to another, so that no reader closes the book under the impression that mercy has been withdrawn from the world.

**WHO IS BEING DESCRIBED, AND WHO DECIDES**

The verse raises a question that every teacher of the Qur'an has to answer. If God already knows who will not believe, does the warning have any meaning? Al-Ṭabarī works through the difficulty with his usual care, and he reports the answer of the early scholars in two steps. God's knowledge of what a person will do does not force the person to do it. The warning is the very thing that makes his choice real, since a person who is warned has been given something to accept or to refuse.

Al-Saʿdī gives the same answer in a sentence: the invitation still stands, and its effect on these people is that it completes the case against them. Ibn ʿUthaymīn put it in a way his students repeated, that a person is not punished for what God knew about him but for what he did, and that what God knew was never a hidden trap. Al-Alūsī records the discussion at length and notes that the Book describes people who refused only after the truth had become clear to them, and never before it.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the verse as the head of a passage. Ibn Kathīr records the map of the passage as Mujāhid drew it: four verses on the believers, two on the people who openly refuse, and thirteen on the people who say one thing and mean another. The next verse explains why the warning does not reach the group named here, and the explanation is one of the most sobering sentences in the Book, because it describes a shut door rather than a locked heart.

Imagine a man walking away from a fire alarm with his hands over his ears. The alarm still works, the building still burns, and the hands belong to the man. Al-Baghawī notes that the verses describe the disbelievers of the earlier books and of the idolaters as one phenomenon, and that the sūrah keeps them in view while it builds the community it is addressing. The Book offers the description as a warning to the reader rather than as a verdict on a stranger, which is why the explanation that follows is written so plainly.
---

## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**WHAT A SEAL IS**

The warning that cannot reach the people described is explained in one line: ***“Allah has sealed their hearts”***. Al-Qurṭubī begins with the language and defines the seal as a covering placed on a thing and made fast, so that nothing enters it and nothing leaves without permission. He gives the examples everyone knows: a sealed letter, a sealed jar, a sealed door. The picture is not of a wound but of a closure, and the thing sealed is the organ with which a person understands.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn explained the word to his students with the seal on a letter. When the writer has finished, the letter is closed and stamped, and the matter is settled. Then he moved to the image of the container of provisions sealed with wax, where the contents are secure and cannot be reached. He observed that the sūrah uses the same construction twice in one line, first for the hearts and then for the hearing, so that the reader understands the sentence as God closing the doors of understanding rather than as God damaging an organ.

Al-Jalālayn reads the seal as an impression made on the heart so that no good enters it, and a deposit placed in the hearing so that the truth a person hears does not benefit him. He then reads the third clause as a veil over the eyes so that the truth is not seen. Al-Saʿdī gives the same reading and calls the three together the closing of the roads of knowledge, which leaves a person with his faculties intact and his access gone.

Imagine a house with a good door, a good window and a working lock, and a man inside who has locked all three himself and then complains that the daylight cannot get in. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the Book describes the heart of the person in question elsewhere as having a covering, a stain, a hardening and a disease, and that each word names a different stage of the same process. The verse in front of the reader is the stage where the process has finished.

**THE THREE DOORS OF UNDERSTANDING**

The line closes two more openings after the heart: ***“and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment”***. Ibn ʿUthaymīn counted the three as the routes by which guidance normally arrives. A person comes to the truth by hearing what is said to him, by seeing what is in front of him, and by understanding what he receives, and the verse reports that all three routes have been closed. He then cited the verse that gathers the same three organs in one sentence: (45:23 — **“sealed their hearing and hearts, and placed a cover on their sight. Who then can guide them after Allah?”**).

Ibn Kathīr records the illustration of Mujāhid, who held up his hand and explained that the heart is like an open palm. A servant commits a sin, and a finger folds; he commits another, and another finger folds, until the hand is closed and the heart is sealed. Mujāhid added that the stain is the first stage, the seal is the second and the lock is the worst. He pointed to the verse that describes a heart spoiled by what its owner used to do: (83:14 — **“their hearts have been stained by all ˹the evil˺ they used to commit”**).

Al-Qurṭubī gathers ten descriptions the Book uses for a heart in this condition, and the list is worth reading in full because no single word carries the whole weight. The heart is described as sealed, stamped, narrowed, diseased, stained, dead, hardened, turned away, filled with pride and finally as denying. He sets the verses beside each other in his commentary, and a reader who follows the list sees a single process told from ten angles, with each angle said to be the patient's own condition rather than a sentence imposed from outside.

Imagine a man who owns an orchard and refuses to prune the trees for ten years, and who then blames the orchard for its fruit. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have identified the group in this verse as named individuals in Madīnah and Makkah, the men who had pushed the invitation away again and again while the invitation was still open. Al-Saʿdī notes the same sequence and calls the closing of the doors a present punishment rather than a distant one, so that a reader meets the consequences of refusal inside his own lifetime before he meets them in the next.

**THE SEAL FOLLOWS A CHOICE**

Al-Saʿdī answers the question the verse raises before a reader can ask it. The sealing came because these people denied after the truth had become clear to them. The Book says as much when it reports that God turns away the hearts of people who did not believe the first time: (6:110 — **“We turn their hearts and eyes away ˹from the truth˺ as they refused to believe at first”**). Ibn ʿUthaymīn used the same verse in his lesson and added another beside it, the report of Moses to a community that kept hurting him: (61:5 — **“when they ˹persistently˺ deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate”**). His conclusion was direct: a person who has gone astray is the cause of his own straying.

Ibn Kathīr notes that the scholars of the community agreed on the fact of the sealing and on the meaning of it. He reports al-Qurṭubī's statement that there is no disagreement among them that God deserves this description of Himself. The disagreement that existed was about how the sealing relates to a person's freedom, and Ibn Kathīr's reading of the verses settles it the way al-Saʿdī does, by keeping the punishment after the crime in the order of the text. The verse does not say God sealed the hearts of people who wanted to believe.

The line ends with the punishment that follows, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the sūrah is careful about the order of its sentences. The condition is described, its cause is described, and then the outcome is named, so that the reader is never given a picture of arbitrary severity. Al-Baghawī records the early teachers saying that the verse is a warning aimed at every reader, since every reader is a person who receives warnings and decides what to do with them.

Imagine two men walking on a road beside a high wall, and one of them keeps climbing the wall and slipping back into the traffic. The other one does not lecture him from the far side of the wall; he keeps walking and keeps calling. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the sūrah leaves this warning and moves at once to a new group of people, the ones whose tongues accept what their hearts have not, and that the reader should notice how much room the Book gives to that group. The verses teach a person to examine his own doors rather than to measure the doors of others. Ibn ʿUthaymīn taught that the one who examines his own hearing, sight and heart every day is unlikely to find them sealed.
---

## Verse 2:13

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:249

> When Saul marched forth with his army, he cautioned: “Allah will test you with a river. So whoever drinks ˹his fill˺ from it is not with me, and whoever does not taste it—except a sip from the hollow of his hands—is definitely with me.” They all drank ˹their fill˺ except for a few! When he and the ˹remaining˺ faithful with him crossed the river, they said, “Now we are no match for Goliath and his warriors.” But those ˹believers˺ who were certain they would meet Allah reasoned, “How many times has a small force vanquished a mighty army by the Will of Allah! And Allah is ˹always˺ with the steadfast.”

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things
